First, to obtain accurate reading result for each video text instance, a context-aggregated instance gathering module is designed to integrate the visual appearance, layout characteristics, and textual contents of the related entities into a unified textual representation. Then, to capture dynamic evolution of text in the video flow, an instance-focused trajectory tracing module is utilized to establish spatio-temporal relationships between instances and infer the final answer. Extensive experiments on several public Video TextVQA datasets validate the effectiveness and generalization of our framework. GAT outperforms existing Video TextVQA methods, video-language pretraining methods, and video large language models in both accuracy and inference speed. Notably, GAT surpasses the previous state-of-the-art Video TextVQA methods by 3.86\% in accuracy and achieves ten times of faster inference speed than video large language models. The source code is available at https://github.com/zhangyan-ucas/GAT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Gather and Trace: Rethinking Video TextVQA from an Instance-oriented Perspective" proposes a new approach to Video Text-based Visual Question Answering (Video TextVQA).  Instead of the traditional frame-level processing, the authors introduce an instance-oriented perspective. Their model, called GAT (Gather and Trace), consists of two main modules: (1) a context-aggregated instance gathering module that integrates visual appearance, layout, and textual content of video text instances, and (2) an instance-focused trajectory tracing module that captures the dynamic evolution of text by establishing spatio-temporal relationships.  The GAT model is evaluated on several public Video TextVQA datasets and demonstrates improvements over existing Video TextVQA methods, video-language pretraining methods, and video large language models in both accuracy and inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in shifting the focus from frame-level processing to an instance-oriented approach for Video TextVQA.  This is a valuable contribution because the frame-level methods often struggle with redundant or low-quality text spotted in individual frames and lack explicit relational modeling between text occurrences across the video.  The two proposed modules are also novel in their design and application to the task. Specifically:

    *   *Context-aggregated Instance Gathering:* Integrates multiple modalities and uses auxiliary losses to handle the dynamic nature of video text, which is a novel approach for creating robust textual representations from noisy video frames.
    *   *Instance-focused Trajectory Tracing:* Explicitly models the spatio-temporal relationships between instances using trajectory distances, which is a more interpretable and effective way to capture the evolution of text in videos compared to implicit spatio-temporal embeddings used in prior work.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance is evident in its performance gains and efficiency improvements compared to state-of-the-art methods.  The fact that GAT surpasses existing Video TextVQA methods and Video-LLMs in both accuracy and inference speed highlights the importance of the proposed instance-oriented approach.  The efficiency gains are particularly relevant as they address a critical limitation of existing methods that rely on lengthy inputs and computationally intensive models. The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the contribution of each component. The qualitative analysis reinforces the benefits of the proposed method by visualizing how it is able to handle noisy and dynamic video text. The reported gains against very strong baselines also highlight the signficance.

* **Strengths:**

    *   The instance-oriented perspective is a valuable conceptual shift in the field.
    *   The two modules (context aggregation and trajectory tracing) are well-designed and contribute to overall performance.
    *   The paper provides strong experimental results, with thorough ablation studies and comparisons to various baselines.
    *   The analysis of efficiency is a strength, demonstrating the practical advantages of the approach.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its contributions.

* **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the individual modules are novel, the overall architecture of GAT still relies on the two-stage paradigm (VTS + Transformer) commonly used in Video TextVQA, albeit improving each stage.
    *   The dependence on GoMatching could be seen as a limitation.  While the authors show improvement *on top* of GoMatching, the performance of GAT is intrinsically linked to the quality of GoMatching. It is not entirely end-to-end in that respect.
    *   The computational cost of GoMatching is not explicitly addressed. While GAT improves the *inference* speed compared to LLMs and prior state-of-the-art approaches, the efficiency of the front-end video text spotting (VTS) step could have been discussed.
    *   There's some reliance on existing components (T5 decoder for autoregressive prediction). While necessary for achieving the results, this does slightly reduce the "core" novelty of the overall framework.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the Video TextVQA field by promoting the instance-oriented paradigm. Other researchers might build upon the GAT architecture and the proposed context aggregation and trajectory tracing modules, or explore entirely different instance-oriented approaches. The efficiency results could spur further research into methods that reduce computational costs while maintaining accuracy.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to Video TextVQA by shifting the focus from frame-level processing to instance-oriented modeling.  The proposed GAT model achieves significant performance gains and efficiency improvements compared to state-of-the-art methods, which demonstrates the value of the proposed approach. While the reliance on a VTS method such as GoMatching and a T5-like Transformer as the generative model reduces some of the novelty, the two core modules are well-designed and contribute significantly to the results. Therefore, considering the conceptual shift, performance improvement, and efficiency gains, a score of **8** is justified.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reasoning Beyond Labels: Measuring LLM Sentiment in Low-Resource, Culturally Nuanced Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04199v1)
- **Authors**: Millicent Ochieng, Anja Thieme, Ignatius Ezeani, Risa Ueno, Samuel Maina, Keshet Ronen, Javier Gonzalez, Jacki O'Neill
- **Abstract**: Sentiment analysis in low-resource, culturally nuanced contexts challenges conventional NLP approaches that assume fixed labels and universal affective expressions. We present a diagnostic framework that treats sentiment as a context-dependent, culturally embedded construct, and evaluate how large language models (LLMs) reason about sentiment in informal, code-mixed WhatsApp messages from Nairobi youth health groups. Using a combination of human-annotated data, sentiment-flipped counterfactuals, and rubric-based explanation evaluation, we probe LLM interpretability, robustness, and alignment with human reasoning. Framing our evaluation through a social-science measurement lens, we operationalize and interrogate LLMs outputs as an instrument for measuring the abstract concept of sentiment. Our findings reveal significant variation in model reasoning quality, with top-tier LLMs demonstrating interpretive stability, while open models often falter under ambiguity or sentiment shifts. This work highlights the need for culturally sensitive, reasoning-aware AI evaluation in complex, real-world communication.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of sentiment analysis in low-resource, culturally nuanced contexts.  It proposes a diagnostic framework for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in understanding sentiment expressed in informal, code-mixed WhatsApp messages from youth health groups in Nairobi.  The core idea is to move beyond simple label prediction and treat sentiment as a context-dependent construct, focusing on how LLMs reason about sentiment. The authors employ a combination of human-annotated data, sentiment-flipped counterfactuals (generated using GPT-4), and rubric-based explanation evaluation to probe LLM interpretability, robustness, and alignment with human reasoning. They investigate how LLMs explain their judgements, handle ambiguity and capture culturally specific nuances.  The paper ultimately argues for a shift towards culturally sensitive, reasoning-aware AI evaluation, emphasizing that high task accuracy alone does not guarantee a robust understanding of sentiment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its diagnostic framework and evaluation methodology, particularly the use of sentiment-flipped counterfactuals generated with GPT-4 and evaluated using a dual human/LLM judge protocol. While previous work has explored sentiment analysis in code-mixed and low-resource settings, this paper offers a more structured and nuanced approach to probing LLM reasoning in such contexts.  The framing of sentiment analysis as a social science measurement problem, requiring careful definition and connection to observable indicators, is also a valuable contribution.  The use of GPT-4 for counterfactual generation is relatively novel in sentiment analysis, although counterfactual techniques themselves are well-established in NLP. However, the framework is primarily an evaluation methodology.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it highlights the limitations of traditional sentiment analysis metrics (accuracy, F1-score) when dealing with the complexities of real-world communication. It underscores the importance of considering cultural context and ambiguity in sentiment understanding. The insights into how different LLMs handle these challenges are valuable for guiding the development of more robust and reliable sentiment analysis systems. The focus on reasoning and interpretability, rather than just classification performance, is a crucial step towards building more trustworthy AI systems. Moreover, the study contributes to an ongoing discussion about how to properly evaluate GenAI model capabilities. However, the scope is limited to a very specific context (WhatsApp health groups in Nairobi).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined methodology:**  The use of counterfactuals, human-LLM judge protocol, and detailed rubrics provide a comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Focus on reasoning and interpretability:**  Moving beyond simple accuracy metrics is a significant strength.
    *   **Culturally grounded:** The study incorporates cultural nuance and contextual understanding.
    *   **Clear articulation of limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of their work, which enhances credibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited generalizability:** The study's focus on a single dataset from a specific context limits the generalizability of the findings.  While the methodology is adaptable, the specific insights about model performance may not hold across different populations or communication styles.
    *   **Dependency on GPT-4 for counterfactual generation:** The quality of the counterfactuals is dependent on the capabilities and biases of GPT-4. While the paper acknowledges this, the lack of independent validation of the counterfactuals is a concern. Additionally, there is an assumption that GPT-4 can generate natural counterfactuals despite being trained primarily on English.
    *   **Lack of causal analysis:** The paper shows differences in model performance but does not deeply investigate *why* certain models struggle in specific cases. A deeper dive into the types of errors made by different models would enhance the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in sentiment analysis by encouraging a more nuanced and context-aware approach to evaluation.  The diagnostic framework and methodology could be adapted and applied to other low-resource, culturally diverse settings. It could also stimulate further research on how to improve LLM reasoning in complex communication scenarios.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**
The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of sentiment analysis, presenting a novel and well-executed diagnostic framework that sheds light on the limitations of existing evaluation methods. Its significance lies in its emphasis on reasoning, interpretability, and cultural context, which are often overlooked in traditional approaches. While the paper's generalizability is limited by its focus on a specific dataset, the methodological contributions and insights into LLM behavior make it a valuable addition to the literature. The limitations (dependency on GPT-4, lack of deeper causal analysis) prevent a higher score, but the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a valuable contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ViFP: A Framework for Visual False Positive Detection to Enhance Reasoning Reliability in VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04201v1)
- **Authors**: Ben Zhang, LuLu Yu, Lei Gao, Jing Liu, QuanJiang Guo, Hui Gao
- **Abstract**: In visual-language model (VLM) reasoning, false positive(FP) reasoning occurs when a model generates a correct answer but follows an incorrect reasoning path. Existing methods based on specific multi-step reasoning datasets and reinforcement learning strategies, leading to high training costs and limited generalization. In this work, we propose ViFP, a general framework for enhancing visual reasoning reliability. It improves both answer accuracy and reasoning soundness by detecting FPs. ViFP tackles the limitations of dataset dependency and poor generalization by constructing sub-question templates grounded in the core dimensions of visual reasoning, such as object localization, characteristic description, and object discovery. ViFP then builds effective reasoning paths via multi-turn QA to improve reasoning accuracy. Meanwhile, ViFP dynamically analyzes the consistency of reasoning path to identify potential FPs, and introduces a targeted chain-of-thought (CoT) mechanism that adaptively guides both FP and non-FP samples. Thereby reducing logical errors in the reasoning path while preserving accuracy. Finally, we introduce a reliability evaluation metric-VoC, which integrates answer accuracy and the FP rate, providing a quantitative tool to assess whether a VLM not only answers correctly, but also reasons reliably. Our experiments on closed-source VLMs show that ViFP consistently improves performance across three datasets: A-OKVQA, OKVQA, and FVQA. On A-OKVQA, ViFP improves accuracy by up to 5.4%, surpassing the previous state-of-the-art by 4.3%, and significantly reduces the number of FPs, validating its benefits in enhancing reasoning reliability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViFP, a framework designed to enhance the reliability of visual-language models (VLMs) by detecting and correcting false positive (FP) reasoning.  ViFP tackles the problem of VLMs producing correct answers through incorrect reasoning paths. It works by constructing sub-question templates based on core dimensions of visual reasoning (object localization, description, discovery), improving reasoning paths via multi-turn QA, dynamically analyzing reasoning path consistency to identify potential FPs, and then applying a targeted chain-of-thought (CoT) mechanism.  The framework aims to improve both answer accuracy and the soundness of the reasoning process. The paper also proposes a new reliability metric, VoC (Value of Correction), integrating answer accuracy and the FP rate. Experiments on closed-source VLMs across A-OKVQA, OKVQA, and FVQA datasets demonstrate consistent performance improvements and FP reduction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its integrated approach to FP detection and correction *within* VLMs, without relying on external, pre-trained supervisory models.  Many CoT approaches try to reduce FP by design. ViFP, on the other hand, explicitly *detects* FPs. The self-detection mechanism is innovative, and the adaptive CoT approach tailored based on detected FPs also presents a novel direction. Furthermore, the introduction of the VoC metric provides a concrete way to quantify the value of correcting FPs.  The construction of sub-question templates based on visual reasoning dimensions is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** Improving the *reliability* of VLMs is crucial for deploying them in real-world applications.  Knowing *why* a model arrives at an answer is often just as important, if not more important, than the answer itself. By addressing FP reasoning, ViFP makes a tangible contribution to the trustworthiness of VLMs. The reported performance gains on multiple datasets and using various closed-source VLMs suggest that the approach is generalizable and has practical value. The VoC metric offers a means for more nuanced evaluation, shifting focus beyond just raw accuracy.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Formulation:**  The paper clearly defines the FP reasoning problem in VLMs and motivates the need for enhancing reliability.
    *   **Methodology:** ViFP is a well-designed and comprehensive framework, with each component (question classification, sub-question generation, CoT construction, FP detection) logically connected.
    *   **Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, using multiple datasets and models. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component. The introduction and use of the VoC metric is a significant strength.
    *   **Results:**  The reported improvements in accuracy and FP reduction are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of ViFP.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Closed-Source VLM Dependency:**  The paper relies heavily on closed-source VLMs. While this demonstrates the applicability of ViFP to powerful models, it limits the reproducibility and accessibility of the research. It would strengthen the paper significantly to evaluate ViFP on more accessible open-source VLMs.
    *   **Lack of Detailed Error Analysis:** While the paper reports improvements, a deeper analysis of the specific types of errors that are corrected and the types of FPs that are most effectively detected would be beneficial.  More concrete examples beyond the two shown in Figure 3 would be good.
    *   **VoC Metric Sensitivity:**  The VoC metric, while promising, might be sensitive to the specific characteristics of different datasets and models. Further analysis of its behavior and limitations would be helpful. The authors noted that the accuracy on OKVQA drops when Gemini-2.5 is used, showing that it's sensitive to the response style, and the authors attribute this to an increase in the number of words it uses compared to other VLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5** to this paper.

*   The **novelty** of the integrated FP detection and correction approach, the adaptive CoT mechanism, and the introduction of the VoC metric warrant a high score.
*   The **significance** of enhancing VLM reliability and the generalizability of the framework across different datasets and models further supports this assessment.
*   While the **dependence on closed-source models and limited error analysis** are weaknesses, they do not outweigh the core contributions of the paper. The authors demonstrate a clever approach to detecting and correcting false positives in VLMs that is demonstrably effective.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DP-DocLDM: Differentially Private Document Image Generation using Latent Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04208v1)
- **Authors**: Saifullah Saifullah, Stefan Agne, Andreas Dengel, Sheraz Ahmed
- **Abstract**: As deep learning-based, data-driven information extraction systems become increasingly integrated into modern document processing workflows, one primary concern is the risk of malicious leakage of sensitive private data from these systems. While some recent works have explored Differential Privacy (DP) to mitigate these privacy risks, DP-based training is known to cause significant performance degradation and impose several limitations on standard training procedures, making its direct application to downstream tasks both difficult and costly. In this work, we aim to address the above challenges within the context of document image classification by substituting real private data with a synthetic counterpart. In particular, we propose to use conditional latent diffusion models (LDMs) in combination with differential privacy (DP) to generate class-specific synthetic document images under strict privacy constraints, which can then be utilized to train a downstream classifier following standard training procedures. We investigate our approach under various pretraining setups, including unconditional, class-conditional, and layout-conditional pretraining, in combination with multiple private training strategies such as class-conditional and per-label private fine-tuning with DPDM and DP-Promise algorithms. Additionally, we evaluate it on two well-known document benchmark datasets, RVL-CDIP and Tobacco3482, and show that it can generate useful and realistic document samples across various document types and privacy levels ($\varepsilon \in \{1, 5, 10\}$). Lastly, we show that our approach achieves substantial performance improvements in downstream evaluations on small-scale datasets, compared to the direct application of DP-Adam.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DP-DocLDM: Differentially Private Document Image Generation using Latent Diffusion Models" addresses the problem of generating synthetic document images while preserving data privacy. The authors propose a novel approach, DP-DocLDM, which combines Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs) with differential privacy (DP) to generate class-specific synthetic document images. This allows for training downstream document image classifiers on synthetic data instead of real private data, mitigating privacy risks. The approach is evaluated across various pretraining setups (unconditional, class-conditional, layout-conditional) and private training strategies (DPDM, DP-Promise) on RVL-CDIP and Tobacco3482 datasets. The results show that the proposed method can generate useful and realistic document samples under strict privacy constraints and achieves performance improvements in downstream tasks compared to directly training with DP-Adam, especially on smaller datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to differentially private document image generation using latent diffusion models. While diffusion models and differential privacy are individually established techniques, their combination for this specific task (and particularly within the context of document image analysis) constitutes a significant contribution. The exploration of different pretraining and private training strategies further enhances the novelty by offering a comprehensive benchmark of various approaches. The use of OCR-based layout conditioning also appears novel and well-motivated.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial and increasingly relevant problem: protecting sensitive information in document processing workflows.  As deep learning models become more prevalent in document analysis, the need for privacy-preserving techniques is paramount. The ability to generate synthetic data that effectively substitutes real private data allows for the development of secure and ethical document processing systems. The significant performance improvements over direct DP training, particularly on small datasets, makes the approach practical and impactful.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly explains the problem, its importance, and the limitations of existing solutions.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The approach is rigorously evaluated across multiple datasets, pretraining setups, and private training strategies. The inclusion of both qualitative and quantitative analysis provides a comprehensive understanding of the method's performance.
    * **Practical Impact:** The results demonstrate that the proposed method can generate realistic and useful synthetic document images while preserving privacy, which has direct implications for real-world document processing applications.
    * **Open Source:**  The public availability of the code promotes reproducibility and further research in the area.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The approach involves multiple stages (pretraining, private fine-tuning, sampling), which can make it complex to implement and tune.  The paper acknowledges the need for careful hyperparameter tuning, which can be a barrier for practitioners.
    * **Dependency on OCR:** The layout conditioning relies on OCR, which may introduce errors and biases, especially for low-quality or handwritten documents. This could limit the applicability of the approach to certain types of documents.
    * **Limited Improvement on Large Datasets:** The gains on RVL-CDIP, a relatively large dataset, are marginal compared to direct DP training. While the improvements on the smaller Tobacco3482 dataset are substantial, the generalizability of these results to other large-scale document datasets needs further investigation. The performance variance on different autoencoders also points to more nuanced dynamics that warrant deeper analysis.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of document image analysis by providing a viable and practical solution for privacy-preserving synthetic data generation. The comprehensive benchmark and open-source code can serve as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working in this area. The approach can be extended to other document analysis tasks, such as layout analysis and information extraction.

**Justification:**

The paper represents a strong contribution to the emerging field of privacy-preserving document analysis. The combination of LDMs with differential privacy for document image generation is novel and addresses a significant problem. The comprehensive evaluation and open-source code further enhance its impact. However, the complexity of the approach, the dependency on OCR, and the limited improvement on large datasets prevent it from achieving a perfect score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hierarchical Text Classification Using Black Box Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04219v1)
- **Authors**: Kosuke Yoshimura, Hisashi Kashima
- **Abstract**: Hierarchical Text Classification (HTC) aims to assign texts to structured label hierarchies; however, it faces challenges due to data scarcity and model complexity. This study explores the feasibility of using black box Large Language Models (LLMs) accessed via APIs for HTC, as an alternative to traditional machine learning methods that require extensive labeled data and computational resources. We evaluate three prompting strategies -- Direct Leaf Label Prediction (DL), Direct Hierarchical Label Prediction (DH), and Top-down Multi-step Hierarchical Label Prediction (TMH) -- in both zero-shot and few-shot settings, comparing the accuracy and cost-effectiveness of these strategies. Experiments on two datasets show that a few-shot setting consistently improves classification accuracy compared to a zero-shot setting. While a traditional machine learning model achieves high accuracy on a dataset with a shallow hierarchy, LLMs, especially DH strategy, tend to outperform the machine learning model on a dataset with a deeper hierarchy. API costs increase significantly due to the higher input tokens required for deeper label hierarchies on DH strategy. These results emphasize the trade-off between accuracy improvement and the computational cost of prompt strategy. These findings highlight the potential of black box LLMs for HTC while underscoring the need to carefully select a prompt strategy to balance performance and cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of black-box Large Language Models (LLMs), accessed through APIs, for Hierarchical Text Classification (HTC). It addresses challenges like data scarcity and model complexity often encountered in traditional HTC methods. The authors evaluate three prompting strategies: Direct Leaf Label Prediction (DL), Direct Hierarchical Label Prediction (DH), and Top-down Multi-step Hierarchical Label Prediction (TMH), in both zero-shot and few-shot settings.  The study compares the accuracy and cost-effectiveness of these strategies across two datasets. Results indicate that few-shot learning generally improves accuracy over zero-shot, DH performs well on datasets with deeper hierarchies, and API costs increase with the complexity of the prompting strategy (especially DH). The research highlights the potential of black-box LLMs for HTC while emphasizing the need to balance performance and cost when selecting a prompting strategy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real-world problem (HTC) and explores a readily accessible alternative (black-box LLMs) to resource-intensive traditional methods. This has immediate practical implications for researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The study provides a well-structured comparative analysis of different prompting strategies. This systematic approach allows for a clear understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of each strategy in different scenarios.
    *   **Cost Analysis:** The inclusion of a cost analysis is crucial. It goes beyond accuracy and acknowledges the financial implications of using LLM APIs, which is vital for real-world applications.
    *   **Data Contamination Check:** The authors perform data contamination checks, a critical step in ensuring that the model performances were not due to the model already having the knowledge of the used data through training.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of LLMs:** The study relies solely on a specific version of OpenAI's GPT-4o. This limits the generalizability of the findings.  The performance and cost trade-offs might vary significantly with other LLMs (e.g., open-source models, models from other providers).
    *   **Hierarchy Depth:** The analysis is limited to datasets with relatively shallow hierarchies (two to three levels). The effectiveness of the prompting strategies, especially DH and TMH, on datasets with deeper and more complex hierarchies remains unclear.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to State-of-the-Art LLM-Based HTC:** The comparison is done against HPT, a Transformer-based non-LLM specific model from conventional research.  While valuable, it doesn't directly compare to other recent LLM-based HTC approaches (besides citing them in the related work). The lack of a direct comparison against those LLM baselines makes the work less impactful.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** While three prompting strategies are evaluated, there's limited discussion on *why* those strategies were chosen and *how* they were optimized. More in-depth prompt engineering (e.g., exploring different prompt formats, adding contextual information) could have yielded better results.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Concerns:** While the use of ACC1, ACC2 and ACC3 along with conditional probabilities is appropriate, other hierarchical evaluation metrics might better capture the performance of the models.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The novelty of the paper lies in its systematic exploration of prompting strategies for HTC with black-box LLMs, with a focus on practical cost considerations. It is significant because it makes LLMs more accessible for HTC tasks, particularly for those with limited resources. However, the limited scope and lack of comparison against state-of-the-art LLM models dilute the impact. The lack of depth in prompt engineering also reduces the paper's overall novelty.

    The paper's strength is in its methodical approach to exploring a relevant and practical problem.  The systematic evaluation of prompting strategies alongside a cost analysis adds value. However, the limitations in LLM choice, hierarchy depth, and benchmark against recent LLM approaches limit the overall impact and novelty.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a moderately significant contribution. It's well-executed within its chosen scope and tackles a practical problem with a clear focus on cost-effectiveness. The systematic exploration of prompting strategies is valuable. However, the limited scope regarding LLMs, hierarchy depth, the lack of a baseline comparison against recent LLM papers and the shallow treatment of prompt engineering hold it back from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. It serves as a useful starting point for further research in this area but does not represent a major leap forward in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Intention Enhanced Diffusion Model for Multimodal Pedestrian Trajectory Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04229v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Liu, Zhijie Liu, Xiao Ren, You-Fu Li, He Kong
- **Abstract**: Predicting pedestrian motion trajectories is critical for path planning and motion control of autonomous vehicles. However, accurately forecasting crowd trajectories remains a challenging task due to the inherently multimodal and uncertain nature of human motion. Recent diffusion-based models have shown promising results in capturing the stochasticity of pedestrian behavior for trajectory prediction. However, few diffusion-based approaches explicitly incorporate the underlying motion intentions of pedestrians, which can limit the interpretability and precision of prediction models. In this work, we propose a diffusion-based multimodal trajectory prediction model that incorporates pedestrians' motion intentions into the prediction framework. The motion intentions are decomposed into lateral and longitudinal components, and a pedestrian intention recognition module is introduced to enable the model to effectively capture these intentions. Furthermore, we adopt an efficient guidance mechanism that facilitates the generation of interpretable trajectories. The proposed framework is evaluated on two widely used human trajectory prediction benchmarks, ETH and UCY, on which it is compared against state-of-the-art methods. The experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves competitive performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces IntDiff, an intention-enhanced diffusion model for multimodal pedestrian trajectory prediction. The core idea is to improve the interpretability and precision of trajectory predictions by explicitly incorporating pedestrian motion intentions into a diffusion-based framework.  The model includes a motion encoder, a motion intention estimator (using a transformer), and a diffusion-based trajectory generator.  The motion intention is decomposed into lateral and longitudinal components, and a classifier-free guidance strategy is used to condition the diffusion model on these intentions.  Experimental results on ETH and UCY datasets show competitive performance compared to existing state-of-the-art methods. The paper also includes ablation studies to demonstrate the contribution of each component.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the explicit incorporation of pedestrian motion intentions *within* a diffusion model for trajectory prediction. While diffusion models have been applied to trajectory prediction before, and intention estimation has been used in other prediction frameworks, the combination is relatively novel. The decomposition of intention into lateral and longitudinal components is a reasonable, albeit not groundbreaking, design choice. The integration of classifier-free guidance is a standard technique, so it does not contribute significantly to the novelty.

* **Significance:** Accurately predicting pedestrian trajectories is crucial for autonomous vehicle safety and efficiency. By incorporating motion intentions, the model aims to produce more interpretable and realistic predictions.  The competitive results on standard benchmarks demonstrate the potential of the approach. The ablation studies provide some insight into the importance of the intention components, but stronger results would strengthen the claims.
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    * Explicit incorporation of intention into a diffusion model.
    * Competitive results on standard benchmarks (ETH/UCY).
    * Inclusion of ablation studies to validate component contributions.
    * Good overall structure.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the concept of incorporating intention is good, the implementation is fairly standard. The intention estimation module relies on fairly simple rules based on velocity and acceleration to *define* intentions. This approach, while necessary due to a lack of explicit intention labels in the datasets, is somewhat simplistic and might not fully capture the complexities of human intention. More sophisticated intention recognition could further improve the model.
    * The performance gains, while competitive, are not overwhelmingly superior to existing methods. The gains are especially questionable when comparing the results with Social-STGCNN and GroupNet on the same dataset. The differences between these models may be too small to be statistically significant given the size of the data used.
    * The intention recognition module and guidance are fairly standard implementations of known techniques (transformer, classifier-free guidance). The paper lacks a deep theoretical contribution or significant algorithmic innovation, the proposed improvements seem rather limited.
    * The results lack substantial improvements in the experiments conducted.
* **Potential Influence:** The work could influence future research in pedestrian trajectory prediction by encouraging the explicit modeling of intentions within generative frameworks. However, given the somewhat incremental nature of the contribution, the influence might be limited. It primarily provides a baseline for future research to improve intention modeling within diffusion models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable extension to the existing literature on trajectory prediction by integrating intention estimation into a diffusion model. The novelty lies mainly in the integration itself, rather than in entirely new algorithmic breakthroughs. While the results are competitive, the performance improvements are not substantial enough to warrant a high score, and the intention modeling component is fairly simplistic. Although the paper provides a reasonable baseline for future investigations, there is limited exploration and optimization of the current models.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### DocVCE: Diffusion-based Visual Counterfactual Explanations for Document Image Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04233v1)
- **Authors**: Saifullah Saifullah, Stefan Agne, Andreas Dengel, Sheraz Ahmed
- **Abstract**: As black-box AI-driven decision-making systems become increasingly widespread in modern document processing workflows, improving their transparency and reliability has become critical, especially in high-stakes applications where biases or spurious correlations in decision-making could lead to serious consequences. One vital component often found in such document processing workflows is document image classification, which, despite its widespread use, remains difficult to explain. While some recent works have attempted to explain the decisions of document image classification models through feature-importance maps, these maps are often difficult to interpret and fail to provide insights into the global features learned by the model. In this paper, we aim to bridge this research gap by introducing generative document counterfactuals that provide meaningful insights into the model's decision-making through actionable explanations. In particular, we propose DocVCE, a novel approach that leverages latent diffusion models in combination with classifier guidance to first generate plausible in-distribution visual counterfactual explanations, and then performs hierarchical patch-wise refinement to search for a refined counterfactual that is closest to the target factual image. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach through a rigorous qualitative and quantitative assessment on 3 different document classification datasets -- RVL-CDIP, Tobacco3482, and DocLayNet -- and 3 different models -- ResNet, ConvNeXt, and DiT -- using well-established evaluation criteria such as validity, closeness, and realism. To the best of the authors' knowledge, this is the first work to explore generative counterfactual explanations in document image analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DocVCE, a novel framework for generating visual counterfactual explanations (VCEs) for document image classification. DocVCE leverages latent diffusion models, classifier guidance, and a hierarchical patch-wise refinement (HPR) strategy to provide actionable insights into model decision-making. The core idea is to identify the minimal but realistic changes needed in a document image to alter its classification, thus explaining *why* a model makes a specific prediction.  The method is evaluated on three document image classification datasets (RVL-CDIP, Tobacco3482, and DocLayNet) and compared against a state-of-the-art approach (LDCE), showing improvements in validity, closeness, and realism of the generated counterfactuals. The paper claims to be the first to explore diffusion-based counterfactual explanations in the context of document image analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting diffusion-based generative models to produce counterfactual explanations *specifically* for document image classification. While diffusion models and counterfactual explanations are not new concepts individually, their combination in this domain appears to be a novel contribution. Furthermore, the proposed Hierarchical Patch-wise Refinement strategy adds a layer of refinement that attempts to generate more interpretable explanations. The use of classifier-free guidance and gradient consensus mechanism is borrowed from other works but the adaptation to document image data and the proposed architecture may allow for higher-quality counterfactual explanations.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from the increasing importance of transparency and explainability in AI-driven document processing. As these systems are deployed in high-stakes environments (e.g., legal, financial), understanding their decision-making processes is crucial. By providing actionable visual explanations, DocVCE can potentially help users understand and correct biases or spurious correlations learned by classification models. Furthermore, visual counterfactual explanations are generally more intuitive and understandable than feature importance maps, which are harder to interpret. However, the applicability of DocVCE is limited to document image classification and does not extend to other information extraction tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of explainability in document image classification and the need for actionable explanations.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The DocVCE framework is thoroughly explained, including the use of latent diffusion models, classifier guidance, and hierarchical patch refinement.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The authors conduct a rigorous qualitative and quantitative assessment of their approach on three different datasets and compared with a strong baseline.
    *   **Actionable Explanations:** The generated counterfactual examples, alongside the difference maps, offer visual and potentially actionable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The generation of counterfactuals using diffusion models can be computationally intensive, potentially limiting its scalability. While the method uses latent diffusion models, the refinement process still requires multiple calls to the classifier model.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The Hierarchical Patch-wise Refinement (HPR) is tailored to documents, which may limit the generalizability of the approach to other image types.
    *   **Subjectivity in Evaluation:** While the quantitative metrics are well-defined, the evaluation of "realism" can be subjective. More rigorous user studies could strengthen the findings.
    *   **Limited novel architectural contribution.** It borrows from other works to allow adaptation to document analysis.
    *  **Baseline Comparison Limitations**. The baselines that are compared against are not specifically optimized for counterfactual explanations of document images.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could encourage further research into generative counterfactual explanations for document understanding tasks. The DocVCE framework could serve as a basis for developing more robust and interpretable document processing systems. Future research could explore ways to reduce the computational cost, extend the approach to other tasks beyond classification, and further improve the realism and actionability of the generated explanations.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to generate visual counterfactual explanations for document image classification. The integration of diffusion models, classifier guidance, and patch-wise refinement is a significant contribution, even though some components are adaptations of existing techniques. The experimental results demonstrate improvements over a state-of-the-art baseline, showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed framework.

However, the paper has weaknesses that limit its overall impact. The computational cost, limited generalizability of HPR, subjectivity in evaluating realism and limited novel architectural contribution somewhat detract from its significance. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects the solid contributions, but also acknowledges the areas where further improvement is needed. It represents a good step forward in explainable AI for document understanding but not a breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DP-GPT4MTS: Dual-Prompt Large Language Model for Textual-Numerical Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04239v1)
- **Authors**: Chanjuan Liu, Shengzhi Wang, Enqiang Zhu
- **Abstract**: Time series forecasting is crucial in strategic planning and decision-making across various industries. Traditional forecasting models mainly concentrate on numerical time series data, often overlooking important textual information such as events and news, which can significantly affect forecasting accuracy. While large language models offer a promise for integrating multimodal data, existing single-prompt frameworks struggle to effectively capture the semantics of timestamped text, introducing redundant information that can hinder model performance. To address this limitation, we introduce DP-GPT4MTS (Dual-Prompt GPT2-base for Multimodal Time Series), a novel dual-prompt large language model framework that combines two complementary prompts: an explicit prompt for clear task instructions and a textual prompt for context-aware embeddings from time-stamped data. The tokenizer generates the explicit prompt while the embeddings from the textual prompt are refined through self-attention and feed-forward networks. Comprehensive experiments conducted on diverse textural-numerical time series datasets demonstrate that this approach outperforms state-of-the-art algorithms in time series forecasting. This highlights the significance of incorporating textual context via a dual-prompt mechanism to achieve more accurate time series predictions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DP-GPT4MTS, a novel dual-prompt large language model framework for time series forecasting that integrates both numerical and textual data. It addresses the limitations of existing single-prompt approaches by using two complementary prompts: an explicit prompt for task instructions and a textual prompt for extracting context-aware embeddings from time-stamped textual data. A tokenizer generates embeddings for the explicit prompt, while a self-attention mechanism refines the textual prompt embeddings. The authors conducted experiments on various textual-numerical time series datasets, demonstrating superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novel Approach:** The dual-prompt mechanism is a novel approach to integrating textual and numerical data in time series forecasting with LLMs. This differs significantly from single-prompt methods, which can struggle to capture relevant textual information effectively.
    * **Addressing a Specific Problem:** The paper tackles the challenge of incorporating textual information in time series data, a problem often overlooked by traditional methods. This is important given the increasing availability of textual data alongside numerical time series in many real-world applications.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a good set of experiments on diverse datasets, comparing DP-GPT4MTS with several strong baselines including Transformer-based architectures and other LLM-based forecasting models. This provides evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    * **Ablation Study:** The ablation study is well-designed and offers insights into the contribution of individual components, highlighting the importance of both the dual-prompt mechanism and the self-attention operation.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework and experimental setup.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Novelty in Components:** While the *combination* of techniques into a dual-prompt architecture is novel, the *individual components* (BERT, self-attention, pre-trained GPT-2) are well-established. The innovation lies primarily in the way these existing components are assembled and utilized.
    * **Scope of Improvement:** Although the paper demonstrates superior performance, the margins of improvement over some strong baselines might not be exceptionally large in every case, which needs to be interpreted with the computational costs related to using LLMs.
    * **Lack of Deeper Analysis:** While the paper presents a performance comparison, it could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of *why* the dual-prompt mechanism is so effective. A more qualitative analysis of the embeddings learned from the textual prompts and their interaction with the explicit prompts could strengthen the paper. This would help to justify the performance improvement through a clearer understanding of the inner workings of the model.
    * **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** While acknowledged, more detail about the sensitivity of the approach to hyperparameters would be beneficial. The lookback window sensitivity is briefly addressed but other parameters might also impact model performance.

* **Significance:**

    * The paper contributes to the growing body of research on applying LLMs to time series forecasting. The dual-prompt mechanism presents a promising direction for effectively incorporating textual information.
    * The framework can be valuable for real-world applications where both numerical and textual data are available, such as finance, supply chain management, and transportation.
    * The paper offers insights into the design of effective prompts for LLMs in multimodal time series forecasting.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper has good potential to influence future work in time series forecasting. The primary strength lies in its novel dual-prompt approach and rigorous experiments demonstrating the method's effectiveness. It addresses a real and growing problem. The limitations, such as using existing techniques, and the limited scope of performance gains, are not substantial enough to negate the paper's contributions. A deeper analysis of the model's behavior and improved hyperparameter sensitivity would have enhanced its impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### T3Time: Tri-Modal Time Series Forecasting via Adaptive Multi-Head Alignment and Residual Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04251v1)
- **Authors**: Abdul Monaf Chowdhury, Rabeya Akter, Safaeid Hossain Arib
- **Abstract**: Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) seeks to model temporal dynamics among variables to predict future trends. Transformer-based models and large language models (LLMs) have shown promise due to their ability to capture long-range dependencies and patterns. However, current methods often rely on rigid inductive biases, ignore intervariable interactions, or apply static fusion strategies that limit adaptability across forecast horizons. These limitations create bottlenecks in capturing nuanced, horizon-specific relationships in time-series data. To solve this problem, we propose T3Time, a novel trimodal framework consisting of time, spectral, and prompt branches, where the dedicated frequency encoding branch captures the periodic structures along with a gating mechanism that learns prioritization between temporal and spectral features based on the prediction horizon. We also proposed a mechanism which adaptively aggregates multiple cross-modal alignment heads by dynamically weighting the importance of each head based on the features. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that our model consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving an average reduction of 3.28% in MSE and 2.29% in MAE. Furthermore, it shows strong generalization in few-shot learning settings: with 5% training data, we see a reduction in MSE and MAE by 4.13% and 1.91%, respectively; and with 10% data, by 3.62% and 1.98% on average. Code - https://github.com/monaf-chowdhury/T3Time/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces T3Time, a novel tri-modal framework for multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF). It combines time, spectral, and prompt-based semantic representations. T3Time uses an adaptive multi-head cross-modal alignment module to dynamically weigh modality-specific embeddings and incorporates a horizon-aware gating mechanism to adapt the influence of temporal and spectral features. Channel-wise residual fusion enhances representational granularity. Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate superior performance compared to state-of-the-art baselines, particularly in long-horizon forecasting and few-shot learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The tri-modal approach, adaptive multi-head cross-modal alignment, and horizon-aware gating are genuinely novel contributions to the MTSF field.  Existing works typically focus on single modalities or use static fusion techniques.
    *   **Performance:** The empirical results consistently demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across multiple datasets and forecasting horizons.  The performance gains, especially in few-shot settings, highlight the generalizability of the proposed approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The thorough ablation studies provide evidence for the effectiveness of each component of T3Time. The impact of removing residual connections is particularly insightful.
    *   **t-SNE Visualization:** The t-SNE visualization offers qualitative evidence to support the claim that T3Time learns robust multimodal representations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:**  The T3Time framework is relatively complex, involving multiple modules and hyperparameters. This might increase the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners looking to adopt it.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:**  The reliance on prompt-based descriptions introduces a degree of subjectivity and potentially limits the applicability of the model if high-quality descriptions are unavailable.  While the Appendix touches upon it, more detail regarding prompt generation would be valuable.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Although the authors claim computational efficiency as a goal, a more direct comparison of computational cost (training time, inference time) with other state-of-the-art methods would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Scope of Datasets:** While the selected datasets are standard benchmarks, the lack of real-world, domain-specific datasets (e.g., from healthcare or finance) limits the assessment of T3Time's practical utility in specialized contexts.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's significance lies in its holistic approach to MTSF. By combining time-series, spectral and semantic information, T3Time addresses limitations of existing modality-isolated approaches.
    *   The few-shot learning results are particularly important. They suggest that T3Time can effectively leverage limited data to build accurate forecasting models, a critical advantage in many real-world scenarios.
    *   The proposed framework offers a solid foundation for future research in MTSF, specifically in representation learning and cross-modal alignment. It demonstrates the effectiveness of combining different perspectives for a better understanding of underlying temporal dynamics.

*   **Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents compelling results and addresses genuine limitations of prior art in MTSF, it does have some practical constraints. The high model complexity and reliance on prompt engineering could limit widespread adoption, even with performance improvements. The choice of the datasets could also have been more targeted. Furthermore, an explicit discussion of computational cost is absent. Given the novelty, performance improvements, and potential impact on future research, but acknowledging the practical limitations and the need for additional exploration in diverse, real-world settings and potential impact on society, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### KVSink: Understanding and Enhancing the Preservation of Attention Sinks in KV Cache Quantization for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04257v1)
- **Authors**: Zunhai Su, Kehong Yuan
- **Abstract**: Key-Value (KV) cache quantization has become a widely adopted optimization technique for efficient large language models (LLMs) inference by reducing KV cache memory usage and mitigating memory-bound constraints. Recent studies have emphasized the importance of preserving the original precision of KVs for the first few tokens to ensure the protection of attention sinks. While this approach has proven effective in mitigating performance degradation, its underlying principles remain insufficiently understood. Moreover, it fails to address the recent discovery that attention sinks can emerge beyond the initial token positions. In this work, we elucidate the underlying mechanisms of attention sinks during inference by examining their role in the cross-layer evolution of extreme activation outliers. Additionally, we provide a comprehensive analysis of the interplay between attention sinks and KV cache quantization. Based on our enhanced understanding, we introduce \textit{\textbf{KVSink}}, a plug-and-play method that effectively predicts sink tokens with negligible overhead, enabling more thorough preservation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KVSink outperforms the existing Preserve-First-N (PFN) strategy, offering more effective preservation of attention sinks during KV cache quantization. Moreover, when applied to the well-established KVQuant method, KVSink further improves perplexity (PPL) and reduces reliance on 16-bit numerical outliers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "KVSink: Understanding and Enhancing the Preservation of Attention Sinks in KV Cache Quantization for LLMs" tackles the problem of performance degradation in Large Language Models (LLMs) caused by aggressive KV cache quantization.  It argues that preserving the original precision of KVs corresponding to "attention sink" tokens is crucial. The paper goes beyond existing "Preserve-First-N (PFN)" strategies by: (1) elucidating the underlying mechanisms of attention sinks as related to cross-layer evolution of extreme activation outliers, (2) providing an in-depth analysis of the interplay between attention sinks and KV quantization, and (3) introducing KVSink, a plug-and-play method that predicts and preserves sink tokens effectively.  KVSink leverages the intrinsic relationship between attention sinks and "stable outliers" to achieve more thorough preservation with negligible overhead, demonstrating improved perplexity (PPL) and reduced reliance on 16-bit outliers compared to PFN and other methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a solid contribution with reasonably strong novelty.  While the idea of preserving KVs for specific tokens isn't entirely new (PFN already exists), the paper makes substantial advancements by:
    *   **Mechanistic Understanding:**  Diving deep into the cross-layer evolution of outliers and relating them to attention sinks provides a richer explanation than previous approaches, which were largely empirical. This is valuable for understanding *why* these preservation techniques work.
    *   **Dynamic Sink Detection:**  Moving beyond the static "first N tokens" approach is important, given that attention sinks can appear later in the sequence. KVSink addresses a key limitation of prior work.  The use of stable outliers as a *proxy* for sink tokens is a clever idea and a significant contribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper includes extensive analysis of the interplay between attention sinks and various quantization methods, which fills a gap in the literature.

*   **Significance:** The problem of KV cache quantization is vital for deploying LLMs efficiently. The work has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   **Improved Performance:**  Demonstrating improved PPL compared to existing methods suggests KVSink is a practical and effective technique.
    *   **Reduced Reliance on Outlier Handling:** By preserving attention sinks better, KVSink reduces the dependence on outlier-aware quantization, which simplifies the overall quantization process.
    *   **Interpretability:**  The insights into the mechanism of attention sinks and their relationship to outliers could lead to more interpretable and controllable LLM compression techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-motivated problem and clear explanation of existing limitations.
    *   Strong mechanistic analysis and justification for KVSink.
    *   Extensive experiments across multiple models and datasets.
    *   Plug-and-play nature of KVSink, making it easy to integrate with existing quantization pipelines.
    *   Good writing quality, facilitating understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Stable Outliers:** The success of KVSink hinges on the reliability of stable outliers as sink indicators.  While the paper provides evidence for this relationship, it's possible that it may not hold across *all* LLM architectures or tasks, presenting a potential limitation. A more detailed analysis of failure modes would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements over PFN are clear, the absolute gains in PPL compared to sophisticated KV quantization techniques (like KVQuant) might appear incremental in certain scenarios.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Parameter Sensitivity:** While experiments are extensive in terms of models and datasets, deeper exploration of parameter sensitivity (e.g., the top-k parameter for stable outlier detection) would be beneficial.

*   **Justification:**
        KVSink offers a well-researched understanding of attention sinks and introduces a tangible technique for their preservation during quantization. However, the novelty, while significant is incremental, and its dependency on stable outlier detection introduces a minor constraint.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### S2M3: Split-and-Share Multi-Modal Models for Distributed Multi-Task Inference on the Edge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04271v1)
- **Authors**: JinYi Yoon, JiHo Lee, Ting He, Nakjung Choi, Bo Ji
- **Abstract**: With the advancement of Artificial Intelligence (AI) towards multiple modalities (language, vision, speech, etc.), multi-modal models have increasingly been used across various applications (e.g., visual question answering or image generation/captioning). Despite the success of AI as a service for multi-modal applications, it relies heavily on clouds, which are constrained by bandwidth, latency, privacy concerns, and unavailability under network or server failures. While on-device AI becomes popular, supporting multiple tasks on edge devices imposes significant resource challenges. To address this, we introduce S2M3, a split-and-share multi-modal architecture for multi-task inference on edge devices. Inspired by the general-purpose nature of multi-modal models, which are composed of multiple modules (encoder, decoder, classifier, etc.), we propose to split multi-modal models at functional-level modules; and then share common modules to reuse them across tasks, thereby reducing resource usage. To address cross-model dependency arising from module sharing, we propose a greedy module-level placement with per-request parallel routing by prioritizing compute-intensive modules. Through experiments on a testbed consisting of 14 multi-modal models across 5 tasks and 10 benchmarks, we demonstrate that S2M3 can reduce memory usage by up to 50% and 62% in single-task and multi-task settings, respectively, without sacrificing accuracy. Furthermore, S2M3 achieves optimal placement in 89 out of 95 instances (93.7%) while reducing inference latency by up to 56.9% on resource-constrained devices, compared to cloud AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces S2M3, a novel split-and-share architecture for distributed multi-task inference on edge devices.  S2M3 addresses the challenges of deploying large multi-modal models on resource-constrained edge devices by splitting models at the functional module level (encoders, decoders, classifiers) and sharing common modules across different tasks to reduce memory footprint.  It proposes a greedy module-level placement algorithm with per-request parallel routing, prioritizing compute-intensive modules to minimize inference latency. The authors demonstrate through experiments on various multi-modal models, tasks, and benchmarks that S2M3 can significantly reduce memory usage without sacrificing accuracy, achieve near-optimal placement, and reduce inference latency compared to cloud-based AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of splitting multi-modal models at the *functional module level* and sharing modules across different *tasks* to reduce redundancy is a significant and practical contribution. While model partitioning and distributed inference are established areas, the *combination* of functional splitting, module sharing *across tasks*, and a *task-aware placement & routing strategy* targeted *specifically* at resource-constrained edge devices constitutes a notable advance. The per-request parallel routing leveraging multi-modal nature is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial problem in the field of edge AI: the difficulty of deploying increasingly large and complex multi-modal models on resource-limited devices.  The proposed solution has the potential to significantly broaden the applicability of edge AI, enabling more sophisticated applications on devices such as smartphones, IoT devices, and other edge computing platforms. Reducing memory usage, inference latency and deployment costs makes it practical to deploy multiple AI tasks on the same edge device.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Approach:** S2M3 leverages pre-trained models, avoiding the need for expensive retraining or fine-tuning on edge devices.  This is a major practical advantage.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The extensive experimental evaluation, with multiple tasks, models, and benchmarks, provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of S2M3. The comparison to cloud computing and other baselines further strengthens the validation. The performance gain for constrained Jetson devices is very promising.
    *   **Well-Defined Problem & Solution:** The problem is clearly articulated, and the proposed solution is well-designed and explained with detailed algorithms and problem formulation.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper offers in-depth analysis of the results including a performance timeline that provides further insight into the performance gains achieved by S2M3.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Greedy Placement Limitations:** While the greedy placement algorithm is effective in many scenarios, it may not always find the absolute optimal solution, especially with a large number of devices, models, and tasks. The paper acknowledges this, however, the scenarios where the greedy approach breaks down should be discussed more extensively.
    *   **Communication Overhead:** Although stated that communication latency is small in their setup, under certain network conditions this may not hold. It is mentioned that adaptive placement can be used to alleviate the issue, however, such method is outside the scope of the paper.
    *   **Dynamic Load Balancing:** The current approach seems to assume a relatively static workload.  Dynamic load balancing across devices based on real-time request patterns and resource availability would be a valuable extension.  The solution only addresses computation time, not power consumption.
    *   **Limited baseline comparison:** The method comparison is limited as the method is the first for edge-based multimodal multitask inference.
*   **Potential Influence:** S2M3 has the potential to significantly influence the field of edge AI, leading to the development of new architectures and algorithms for deploying multi-modal models on resource-constrained devices.  It could also inspire new research on adaptive resource management, task scheduling, and model partitioning techniques for edge computing environments.

**Justification of Score:**

S2M3 presents a well-motivated, practical, and well-evaluated solution to a significant problem in edge AI. While it has some limitations related to greedy placement and dynamic load balancing, its novelty in functional module splitting and sharing, per-request parallel routing, its comprehensive evaluation, and its potential impact on the field warrant a high score.

- **Score**: 8/10

### A Few Words Can Distort Graphs: Knowledge Poisoning Attacks on Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04276v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi Wen, Tianxin Chen, Zhirun Zheng, Cheng Huang
- **Abstract**: Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) has recently emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing large language models (LLMs) by converting raw text into structured knowledge graphs, improving both accuracy and explainability. However, GraphRAG relies on LLMs to extract knowledge from raw text during graph construction, and this process can be maliciously manipulated to implant misleading information. Targeting this attack surface, we propose two knowledge poisoning attacks (KPAs) and demonstrate that modifying only a few words in the source text can significantly change the constructed graph, poison the GraphRAG, and severely mislead downstream reasoning. The first attack, named Targeted KPA (TKPA), utilizes graph-theoretic analysis to locate vulnerable nodes in the generated graphs and rewrites the corresponding narratives with LLMs, achieving precise control over specific question-answering (QA) outcomes with a success rate of 93.1\%, while keeping the poisoned text fluent and natural. The second attack, named Universal KPA (UKPA), exploits linguistic cues such as pronouns and dependency relations to disrupt the structural integrity of the generated graph by altering globally influential words. With fewer than 0.05\% of full text modified, the QA accuracy collapses from 95\% to 50\%. Furthermore, experiments show that state-of-the-art defense methods fail to detect these attacks, highlighting that securing GraphRAG pipelines against knowledge poisoning remains largely unexplored.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "A Few Words Can Distort Graphs: Knowledge Poisoning Attacks on Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation of Large Language Models" identifies and demonstrates a critical vulnerability in Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) systems.  The authors propose two knowledge poisoning attacks (KPAs) – Targeted KPA (TKPA) and Universal KPA (UKPA) – that can significantly degrade GraphRAG performance by subtly modifying existing text in the knowledge base. TKPA exploits graph topology to target specific queries, while UKPA disrupts the overall graph structure by manipulating linguistic cues like pronouns and coreference.  Experiments show that these attacks are effective, stealthy, and can circumvent existing defenses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and rigorously demonstrating the *manipulation-only attack surface* on GraphRAG.  While previous work like GRAGPOISON focuses on *adding* malicious content, this paper shows that even minor modifications to existing, trusted content can be devastating. This is a significant distinction and highlights a previously unexplored vulnerability specific to GraphRAG's reliance on LLMs for graph construction from text. The two attack strategies (TKPA and UKPA), while building upon existing concepts in graph theory and NLP, are novel in their application and design to specifically target the GraphRAG pipeline.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its practical implications for the security and reliability of GraphRAG systems. As GraphRAG gains traction in knowledge-intensive applications (question answering, dialogue, etc.), the ability to subtly poison the knowledge graph presents a severe threat. The authors thoroughly demonstrate that these attacks are effective and can circumvent existing defenses, revealing substantial security vulnerabilities. Highlighting the vulnerabilities and the ease with which GraphRAG pipelines can be compromised is important. The work underscores the need for more robust defense mechanisms that consider not only the integrity of retrieved chunks but also the process by which knowledge graphs are constructed.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of manipulation-only attacks in GraphRAG and highlights the limitations of previous additive attack models.
    *   **Well-Designed Attacks:** TKPA and UKPA are well-designed and motivated by theoretical insights from graph theory and NLP.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive, using multiple datasets and comparing against relevant baselines and defenses.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of different attack components and parameters.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Stealthiness Metric:** While the paper claims stealthiness, it doesn't have a specific quantifiable metric for it beyond stating the small percentage of text modified. A human evaluation of the modified text or a comparison of perplexity scores might strengthen this claim.
    *   **Limited Defense Evaluation:**  While demonstrating the ineffectiveness of some existing defenses is valuable, exploring some potential defenses specifically tailored to these manipulation attacks could have been a next step.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   **Stimulate Research:** The paper will likely stimulate further research on attack and defense methods for GraphRAG.
    *   **Inform System Design:** The findings can inform the design of more secure GraphRAG pipelines, prompting developers to prioritize the security of graph construction.

*It is important to note that the findings are based on the assumption that LLMs are used to automatically construct knowledge graphs. This is a common approach, but manual construction is another viable approach, albeit more labor intensive.*

**Justification:**
The paper presents a valuable contribution by highlighting a previously underexplored vulnerability in GraphRAG systems. The attacks are well-designed, and the experimental results are convincing. The work has practical implications for the security and reliability of these systems. While it lacks a specific stealthiness metric and provides limited defense evaluation, the overall impact of the findings is significant. It opens up a new avenue of research in attack and defense methods for GraphRAG.
